Thus the mudrās arising from the fist holding one's own emblem and from the vajra dance, the vajra rite-performers that accomplish the hundred groups of rites of the first thousand, are the third. Thus the karma mudrās that accomplish all rites with one or the other of the right and left hands are the fourth thousand. Thus the binding of all mudrās without exception is accomplished by binding all the mudrās with a single hand.

Thus, for the sake of all beings,
Together with retinue, samaya, and rites,
The All-Supreme, the sublime Īśvara,
Was accomplished by Vajrasattva.

Thus this vast and marvelous secret,
Which joins all beings to the supreme,
The unsurpassed highest of the Paramādya,
The clear tantra, I have rightly explained.

From Śrī Paramādya, the King of Ritual Manuals, this is the tantra of the mudrās of all the families.

Then the Teacher, the Vajra Holder,
The steadfast lord of all secrets,
Spoke this sublime weapon
Of the great tantra of the mudrās of reality itself.

Then the Blessed One, the Supremely Secret, said: "I shall explain the supreme host of rites of the secret maṇḍala of all the rites of the Blessed One Vajrasattva: skillful and subtle, vast and profound, inconceivable and immeasurable, clear and unequaled, the unsurpassed highest, bringing about the secret of endless accomplishment, the accomplisher of the rites of all accomplishments, complete and without remainder, unfailing and unobstructed."

Holding the lower lip with the teeth, with great joy, gazing with the gaze of the primal vajra, through these actions one accomplishes all the hosts of rites of all accomplishments, complete and without remainder. This is the mudrā called Vajra Eye, the great secret vajra of Great Bliss.

Laughing with the great laughter of all the tathāgatas, through steadfast, vajra-like actions one accomplishes the rites of all the tathāgatas. This is the mudrā called the Eye of All the Tathāgatas.

With a wrathful frown, the agent endowed with the wrathful vajra gaze, through the syllable hūṃ of the great breath of the nose, is the accomplishment of all vajras. This is the mudrā called the Eye of the Gaze Blazing with Fire like the Vajra Sun.

Then, smiling slightly, through the actions of the vajra gaze of the wisdom of the rites of all accomplishments, one accomplishes the hosts of wisdom of the rites of all accomplishments, such as the vajra of the samādhi of even lips. This is the mudrā called the Great Lotus Eye.

Through a samādhi accompanied by pride, through the actions of a subtly smiling gaze, with a smiling face and with laughter, one accomplishes in the sky the host of rites of all accomplishments. This is the Great Vajra, the mudrā called the Jewel Eye.

Unfailing, unobstructed accomplishment,
The very nature of truth, without fear:
All the buddhas and I as well
Bestow the excellent accomplishment of the secret mudrās,
Even on one who has not entered the maṇḍala.

Then, for that reason, Paramādya,
The Blessed One, supreme Vajrasattva,
First, the exceedingly skillful, subtle, profound, vast, and inconceivable:

The supreme of the supreme, the first,
Supremely secret and sublime,
The host of mudrās such as the smile
Was spoken by Vajrasattva.

The great Vajradhara, the master,
Supremely clear and full of delight,
Even without the heart mantras and the rest,
Swiftly accomplishes everything.

Heart mantra, mudrā, mantra, and vidyā:
This practice is marvelous.
Since it fully accomplishes all accomplishments,
Even what was not accomplished will be accomplished.

If one has not attained accomplishment
And is not endowed with abundant merit,
If one practices the sublime self, Vajrasattva,
It will swiftly be accomplished.

If, though one has practiced
Supreme Vajrasattva
With the heart mantra and the rest and the near-heart mantra,
It is not accomplished,
One should practice with the smile mudrā.

For this reason this secret consort
Brings great delight to Vajrasattva.
Vajrasattva has great joy,
And through that delight it is swiftly accomplished.

This great reality, which is accomplished,
The very nature of the sublime and supreme,
Supremely secret, endowed with merit,
Is not to be told to ordinary people.

Whatever accomplishment of Vajrasattva
Belongs to one who follows the accomplishment of that reality,
If one desires the supreme accomplishment,
One should not likewise give it to others.

To one who has not become accomplished,
To him one should give that reality.
By that very smile, swiftly,
It will be accomplished through blessing oneself.

This great and pure reality
Is the supreme weapon of the Vajra Bearer.
The unsurpassed tantra of mudrās,
Supremely secret, has been explained.

To the vajra master, all-supreme,
Who follows that reality with faith,
If one desires the supreme accomplishment,
One should likewise give it to others too.

Whoever possesses
This single tantra of mudrās
Cannot be overcome or slain
By Māra and the others.

For him the Victor, the Vajra Holder,
The All-Supreme, is asked for every accomplishment;
Asked for all, doing all, in every way,
All his commands will be accomplished.

This is the ritual of the unsurpassed, supremely secret mudrās of the samaya of the very glory of the holy, supreme master of secrets, Śrī Paramādya.

Then the Blessed One Vajrasattva said: "I shall explain the paramādya of the Blessed One Śrī Paramādya Vajrasattva: supremely skillful, subtle, profound, and vast, inconceivable, endless and utterly limitless, immeasurable and incalculable, unsurpassed, unfailing and unobstructed, the supreme heart of reality, the supremely secret heart, called the supremely secret; the sublime and unsurpassed secret of the host of rites of all accomplishments, such as lordship of the great supreme accomplishment of all, which supremely never changes; that which accomplishes all supreme joys and the supreme accomplishment of bliss and happiness of mind; and the tantra of the supreme mudrās."

All the mudrās of the supreme Blessed One and of Vajrasattva, such as kissing and embracing, are what is called the yoga of the samaya of the joy of Great Joy, glorious Vajra Amogha; it is what is declared as the yoga of all. Wherever it is applied, there the Blessed One,

Maheśvara of the great accomplishment of the All-Supreme,
The god who surpasses the gods,
Having made the sign of supreme blessing,
Vajrasattva is swiftly accomplished.

Even merely by sealing with touch, such as kissing and embracing, it accomplishes the host of rites of all accomplishments, complete, without remainder and without residue, such as the very nature of Maheśvara, supreme of all, fulfilling the desires of the mind. This is the utterly and supremely secret mudrā.

The mudrā of gazing with the corner of a smiling and moving eye, together with playful display: by sealing with what is called the arrow of Vajra Amogha, the great joy of the Blessed One, one accomplishes the host of rites of all accomplishments desired by the mind, such as making all enamored, summoning, bringing under control, and the supreme accomplishment.

The gaze of eyes that smile and are slightly open is the mudrā called Glorious Vajra Eye of supreme joy, which accomplishes everything; with it one should gaze and drink. This is the mudrā called the Supreme Accomplishment of Rejuvenation through the Joy of Glorious Vajra Amogha, the great and utter joy of the Blessed One. By it, even all whose lifespan is exhausted obtain the youthful age of Vajrasattva, freedom from illness, beauty of form, strength, the power of splendor, vigor, all the supreme accomplishments, glory and good fortune, great joy, all utter bliss, happiness of mind, and the supreme accomplishment. For as long as it lasts, the most sublime host of rites of all the supreme accomplishments is accomplished; merely by the sealing of the Blessed Mother it is accomplished in a single instant.

The left eye, with pride, laughing and raised, is the mudrā called the Armlet of the Supreme Victory Banner of Joy of Glorious Vajra Amogha, the great delight of the Blessed One. Sealing with it makes one victorious over all, makes them bow in homage, and thoroughly accomplishes; for as long as everything supreme is accomplished, it thoroughly accomplishes lordship of the great accomplishment.

The gaze with coquetry, endowed with a smile, with candor and with bowing, is the Blessed One's mudrā of homage among the mudrās; it is the mudrā called the ki li ki la of glorious Amogheśvara of Great Joy. Sealing with it all things stable and moving, Vajrasattva and the rest, they bow down, and then one will be accomplished through the sublime and supreme accomplishment of the host of rites of all accomplishments, such as all the supreme accomplishments.

The smiling face itself, with playful display and coquetry, is the supremely secret vajra smile mudrā called Striving through Joy, of Glorious Vajra Amogha, the great joy of the Blessed One Paramādya Vajrasattva. With it, the song of Paramādya, master of all the blessed Vajra Holders, is:

The great accomplishment of the All-Supreme,
Maheśvara, the god who surpasses the gods,
King of all the Vajra Holders:
The unchanging supreme will be accomplished.

Merely by sealing with the coquettish smile mudrā with playful display, it accomplishes the host of rites of all accomplishments desired by the mind, such as the lordship of Maheśvara, of the great accomplishment of all.

Having recited sarba kuru yathā sukhaṃ, this is the mudrā of all the tathāgatas of the Blessed Vajrapāṇi, king of the lords of the secret Mahāyāna sūtras of all the tathāgatas. This one, called the Secret Vajra Blessing of the Full Awakening of the Mahāyāna of All the Tathāgatas, because it is luminous by nature, accomplishes the hosts of rites of all accomplishments, such as the wisdom that attains the dharmatā of all the tathāgatas, complete and without remainder, the complete purity of all phenomena and so on, the higher knowledges, and the supreme accomplishment.

The mudrā of smiling with playful display, with a wrathful frown of the brows and the vajra gaze, is the mudrā called the Blessed One's wrathful ki li ki la. Sealing with it accomplishes the hosts of rites of all indestructible accomplishments: making enamored all things stable and moving, Vajrasattva and the rest; summoning; bringing under control; bringing down; causing to enter; dance; song; displaying magical display; all words of truth; liberation and the accomplishment of all divinatory descents; displaying each of all illusions and all marvels; showing deeds through all various forms; sealing all; harming; taming; killing; turning all back; accomplishing all; and the supreme accomplishment and the rest.

Through the samādhi of the purity of all,
The form of the sameness of all
Fully enters into various deeds
And blesses the offering.

The one called Vajra Lotus is the mudrā of the Blessed One, noble Avalokiteśvara. Through the samādhi of all the tathāgatas it brings about the limitless accomplishments that fulfill aims, such as purifying all, protecting, thoroughly cleansing, pacifying all fevers, poisons, concocted poisons, grahas, illnesses, and the mudrās of the ḍākinīs, fully restoring the dead to life, obtaining all mastery, and fully entering into all deeds of various forms.

Moving subtly through the sky, playing, one should make all yogas enamored. Here, this is the mudrā of homage called the Vajra Blessing of the Blessed Ākāśagarbha. When one seals with it, the Blessed One is enamored and delighted; and being firmly enamored, because he is the very nature of all space, the very substance of all, it fulfills all intentions and accomplishes the hosts of rites of all accomplishments desired in the mind, such as the supreme accomplishment. This too was spoken.

Paramādya, as the supreme itself,
Has spoken the sublime secret:
The sealing with the mudrās of the eyes and the rest.

Was spoken by Vajrasattva.

Through the kissing, embracing, and so on
Of the master who holds all vajras,
Having practiced all the yogas in turn,
One should swiftly seal them all.

This practice of heart mantra, mudrā,
Mantra, and vidyā is marvelous:
Even for those without accomplishment
It fully accomplishes all accomplishments.

One who has no accomplishment
And who lacks abundant merit,
Being Vajrasattva himself, or sealed,
Will swiftly be accomplished.

If, with supreme Vajrasattva,
With the heart mantra and the rest and the near-heart mantra,
What is to be accomplished is not accomplished,
One should seal with kissing and the like.

Because the secret mudrā
Is the sublime bliss of all accomplishments,
The supreme offering to Vajrasattva,
Through it one will swiftly be accomplished.

This great reality, which is accomplished,
Is the holy nature of the supreme,
Supremely secret, endowed with merit;
It is not to be told to ordinary people.

Vajra master, to the supreme disciple
Who follows that reality with faith,
If you desire the supreme accomplishment,
Give it; do not give it to others.

Whoever possesses
This tantra of mudrās alone
Cannot be overcome or slain
By Māra and all the rest.

Then the King, the Vajra Holder,
The All-Supreme, granting all accomplishments,
Doing all and knowing all,
Brings all to accomplishment.

So spoke the Blessed One Vajrasattva. From Śrī Paramādya, the mudrā tantra called the Supremely Secret, the tenfold mudrā, is complete.

Then the Blessed One Vajrasattva of supreme accomplishment said: "This is the explanation of the mudrā tantra called Supreme Accomplishment: supremely skillful, subtle, profound, and vast, inconceivable, utterly limitless, immeasurable, incalculable, inexpressible, and all-pervading."

There, the ten mudrās generate the self of all. The great mudrā of Vajrasattva arises from the samaya of Vajra Amogha of Great Bliss. First of all, it swiftly accomplishes the host of rites of all accomplishments, such as all the first and supreme accomplishments. Here, this is the body mudrā: binding the mudrā of the three banners and the khaṭvāṅga, the left holding a bell and the right held in the manner of raising up, with a dance that releases the fingers and so on with coquetry, Vajrakāmeśvarī is praised. Through the supreme mudrā of Maheśvara, the great accomplishment supreme of all, it is called the Yogeśvarī of Accomplishment, the samaya of delight of glorious Vajra Amogha of Great Joy. When it fully releases the Vajra Proud One, it becomes the four accomplishments. If one seals with that very Vajra Proud One, it is the unchanging supreme state of Maheśvara, the great accomplishment supreme of all. If one seals with the drawing of the arrow, even the vajra goddesses are made enamored, so what need is there to mention other women? If one seals with the vajra ki li ki la, one makes all the women among all the women of the gods and asuras play, and even if it is not accomplished, one is able to accomplish everything. With this mudrā of raising the makara banner, one is victorious over all and kills. Merely by sealing with the mantra and mudrā of Vajrakāmeśvarī laughing fully and casting the garland, one attains mastery over all desires. The mudrā of the supremely secret painting should be explained just as it is. Through the offering of the samaya of Vajra Amogha of Great Bliss, one inexhaustibly obtains all offerings, such as utter joy. With the mudrā of vajra praise, one sets in motion all the three realms of existence and brings them under control. With the mudrā of Vajrahūṃkāra and with the foot, one presses down and seals an image of a god and makes the threatening gesture; by sealing those beings, gods and others, before long they will carry out all commands and rites. Seated on a high seat in the half-crossed posture, pressing down with the left foot an image of Vajrasattva as well, brandishing the vajra and pressing down Vajrasattva, if one experiences all delightful accomplishments and excellences, what need is there to mention gods and other beings? Holding one's own emblem with the left vajra fist, and holding the vajra and so forth in the right in the manner of raising up, one seals all the daughters of the gods whom the mind desires, and by force makes them play, or accomplishes them. This is the great mudrā of Vajrasattva.

Then the samaya mudrās, which arise from the samaya of Vajra Amogha of Great Bliss, are ten: drawing the nails across the heart; striking the hook; inserting the right into the left hook; squeezing the tips of the hooks; the mutual rubbing done at the heart; extending the tongues evenly; placing the vajra firmly at the heart; acting with the vajra; and also the mudrā of play. Through these one unfailingly obtains the accomplishments of all: of accomplishment, excellence, bliss, mudrā, samaya, maṇḍala, Dharma, wisdom, karma, and great bliss.

Then the ten rites arising from the vajra bond of the secret samaya: by the hook of the middle fingers. Within the joined palms, the vajra fingers are the rite of all. The thumb vajra; with it, rubbing on both palms. The index finger vajra; with it, the two hooks. The middle finger vajra; with it one should bind the fingertips. The ring finger vajra; with it, the music of the fingertips. The little finger vajra; with it, squeezing the nails of the two thumbs. By sealing with these one accomplishes all things, Vajrasattva and the rest: making enamored, bringing under control, summoning, play, binding with passion, making all speak, making them dance, making them laugh, and killing.

Then the ten mudrās of the karma samaya: the vajra dance offering. Making all the daughters of the gods and asuras and all young maidens firmly enamored is done by the vajra gaze.

The practice of utter joy is tasting and drinking by moving the lower lip inward. Making passion firm, and the descent, is done by releasing a long breath. Then attaining mastery over all desired rites is done by raising both shoulders. Becoming worshiped by all is done by placing them at the heart. Finding all that exists is done by summoning with the eyes. Arousing desirous passion is done by gazing with a lowered face, together with the middle. The rite of bringing down all is done through the actions of subtly moving one's own heart. Slaying desirous passion is done by making oneself rise through releasing the breath. Performing all rites, such as enjoying all desires, is done by commanding. If these are applied as well to all things stable and moving, Vajrasattva and the rest, then when they are sealed by Amoghasiddhi, in a single instant, with firm and unchanging accomplishment, they will be accomplished. So spoke the Blessed One Vajrasattva.

Then the ten karma mudrās. With the mudrā of the Vajra Proud One, all are accomplished. With the Vajra Weapon Lady come victory over all and making enamored. With the vajra ki li ki la, the touch of utter joy and all accomplishments are blissfully accomplished. By raising the banner comes victory in all battles. With Vajrakāmeśvarī comes great lordship itself. With the offering of the seasons one becomes worthy of worship. By brandishing the vajra, all are tamed. By casting the vajra, all are slain and all are seized. With the left held in pride and through the actions of the right vajra fist, one accomplishes all rites, such as illusion, all miraculous powers, displaying them one by one, stupefying, paralyzing, putting to sleep, making to wander, expelling, causing pustules, inflicting downfall, and bringing under control.

The ten samaya mudrās of Vajrasattva arise from the vajra palms. The vajra supreme body: all accomplishments. With the hook of the two index fingers: all accomplishments. With Vajrayakṣa, which tames the malevolent such as all the māras, that same one covered by the thumb vajra: finding all. That same one with the two thumbs inserted, the uṣṇīṣa vajra: protecting all. By that same one with the thumb vajra inserted within, one accomplishes making enamored, summoning, and bringing under control all beings, Vajrasattva and the rest. The two tips of the two little finger vajra fingers well held by the two index fingers, with the thumb: summoning glory and bringing under control. That same one, the queen of Vajrayakṣa: seizing all wealth and glory. That same one, with the two middle fingers fully squeezing the tips of the two ring fingers: liberation from grahas such as the māras, and, having brought them under control, applying them to all rites. With the vajra of utter joy one accomplishes the supreme of all objects.

Then the secret mudrās arise from the vajra of utter joy. Pressing down the sattva, one makes the vajra goddess play. That too is done by sealing with the great vajra of utter joy. It is done by the vajra of utter joy together with the vajra. It is done by the wheel of all the tathāgatas free of passion: one raises the wheel of the index, middle, and ring fingers, and, with the thumbs and little fingers even, one should spread them apart; it will be sealed and accomplished by that very reality of accomplishing the vajra of utter joy. By squeezing, one fully accomplishes all desires. With the index finger hook one summons the bliss of all desires. With the index finger one fully pacifies the suffering of all desires, such as jealousy. By constantly moving, one brings down utter joy and then accomplishes. By moving the place of birth, in the utter joy of women and men, there is the utterly limitless great bliss of all desires that transcend the world. By squeezing the union, there is also no separation from union for as long as one lives. By rubbing the nail of the sign on the left palm, one becomes attached with utterly firm desire. All women will be accomplished who do not look to the desire of another, do not go to another, and give utter bliss and happiness of mind. By rubbing the nail at the place of birth, it is so for men.

Then the ten secret mudrās of the self of all. There, the mudrā of the whole body: embracing with the two vajra fists, or holding with lips and teeth. Through rapture, one makes them give bliss. Through churning, Vajrasattva will be thoroughly accomplished through the accomplishment of utter joy. Through the accomplishment of Paramādya, saying surata stāṃ to the Blessed One and urging reality, it will be accomplished. Through the wisdom of utter joy, all is accomplished. If the fist of the left hand touches the secret place, all is accomplished. Through the gaze of the Great Vajra arising from the vajra of great bliss, all is accomplished. By raising the eyebrows upward, the descent is drawn up. By tasting one's own lower lip inward, great good fortune is accomplished. By moving one's own tongue, one speaks all one's own truths and reality clearly and without deceit. By bringing down into one's own body, one transforms all forms: one even becomes Vajrasattva or a buddha. By meditating on one's own self as vajra, one travels through the sky.

Then, first, the ten mudrās of all rites: by the vajra palms, the khaṭvāṅga. All accomplishments, by the vajra fists. All accomplishments, by the tathāgata fists. Slaying all, by the wrathful fists. Joining all, by the lotus fists. Seizing all wealth, by the jewel fist. Bringing down all, by the fist that brings down all. Making all dance, by the fists that scatter the khaṭvāṅga. Making all speak, by the tongue fists with even index fingers. Moving all, by the stable fists. Causing all to enter and accomplishing unfailingly, by the vajra hook fist.

Then, with the two vajra fists, there are the ten mudrās of the fingers of the hands, formed as one wishes, which bring accomplishment unfailingly, without obstruction, swiftly, in an instant. They are these: the lute, the flute, the clay drum, the mukuṇḍa, the cymbals, the great drum, the round drum, the paṭaha drum, the guñja, and the timili. When one seals with these, then in every case there is attraction, subjugation, bringing down, release from fevers, poisons, compounded poisons and grahas, slaying, victory in battle, and the attainment of every excellence. Thus it was said.

Because great desire is completely pure,
It is the great Vajrasattva.
The rite of the mudrās has been thoroughly examined.
For the supreme disciple, the vajra master,
To one who follows with faith in this very reality,
By mere understanding, in an instant,
Grants with ease the unfailing accomplishment of all.

So it is said.

The tantra of the mudrās called Supreme Accomplishment, from Śrī Paramādya, is complete.

Then the Blessed One Vajrasattva said: "I shall explain the thousand mudrās of all the assemblies that arise from the vajra dance, called the Supremely Excellent Accomplishment, the assembly of rites of all accomplishments, beginning with the very nature of the great supreme accomplishment, supremely skillful, beyond all extremes, immeasurable and incalculable, entire and without remainder."

Having joined the great mudrā evenly,
By releasing it with grace, with smiles and the rest,
The hundred great action mudrās
Should all be accomplished through dance.

With the secret hook of the vajra of Great Bliss, the binding of the action mudrās in particular: this first hundred, supremely accomplished, are the mudrās of the vajra samaya goddesses.

Having bound the vajra bond,
Shining within in union,
Of the great secret of Paramādya
The action mudrās are another hundred.

These are the mudrās of all the secret vajra goddesses.

With the palms of the great offering of vajra action
Held at the crown of the head,
Since the action mudrās are accomplished,
One should join them with the senses.

These are the mudrās of the goddesses of the great offering of all the vajras.

Dancing as the Vajra Proud One,
Releasing with grace the little finger and the rest,
Binding that very one and dancing,
This latter one arises.

These are the hundred supreme mudrās of Maheśvara, the accomplishment of the samaya of the unfailing action of all the vajras.

Having joined the vajra palms evenly,
All should be accomplished.
This is another supreme mudrā of the great action of Paramādya.

These are the mudrās of all the queens.

Having bound the five arrows gathered together,
The hundred action mudrās of bliss,
Without releasing the supreme delight,
Are applied in bliss itself.

These are the mudrās of the goddesses of desire, the supremely secret ones of all the vajras.

Through the vajra of body, speech and mind,
By joining the supreme mudrās just as they are,
The hundred various action mudrās
Should be discerned as Paramādya.

These are the mudrās of the vajra goddesses of body, speech and mind of all the vajras.

With the khaṭvāṅga of the vajra goddess,
One dances with the whole body
And sings the song of the supreme vajra dance:
This is called the first great mudrā.

As was taught at length above,
The assembly of mudrās of supreme music:
Playing music and singing songs,
The hundred manifestations of all the mudrās.

These are the mudrās of the assembly of the divine maidens of all the vajras, and thus the thousand mudrās are supremely accomplished.

These and other supreme ones without end,
Equal and unequal to the dharmadhātu,
Countless as the reaches of space:
The queen of mudrās grants supreme accomplishment.
For the supreme accomplishment of Maheśvara,
Of the great accomplishment of the All-Supreme,
Through the openings of all of space
They fully enter into this maṇḍala.
It brings about supreme and excellent accomplishment.
The supreme and excellent great tantra
Grants supreme and excellent accomplishment.
The vajra master should teach this way of all the mudrās
To the supreme disciple
Who follows with faith in this very reality,
And not to others.

Thus the supremely unchanging bliss of excellent accomplishment,
Supremely pure, the vajra accomplishment of bliss, endowed with fruit without end,
Is the way of the supreme body of the secret reality of Śrī Paramādya.
This way of the supremely permanent rite is brought to its complete conclusion.

The tantra of the mudrās called Supremely Excellent Accomplishment, from Śrī Paramādya, is complete.

Then all the great beings,
The supreme ones, together with the Vajra Holders,
And the sugatas, made request
To the protector, the great Vajradhara, the chief:

"For the full accomplishment of the lord of accomplishment
Of the all-supreme great space,
The reality that gathers together all the rites
Has been spoken by the Great Being.
Among phenomena that are free of conceptual thought,
What is this meaning of 'rite' that is spoken of?
By what means, in this later tantra,
Is there the supreme state of the first?
Likewise, how are Vajrasattva himself,
Supreme delight itself and excellence itself,
And the self of the Vajra Proud One?
How are mind, being and samādhi,
Likewise vajra and the Vajra Holder,
The great Vajradhara himself,
And the conduct of awakening?
How are Māra and victory over Māra,
Awakening and the Buddha?
How are the fierce vajra, Antaka himself,
And Vajrapāṇi?
How did the first family arise,
And how is desire liberation?
How are the three kinds of protector,
And how are you all things?
How did the first heart essence arise,
And how is what is called the near heart?
How is the unfailing samaya
Of Great Bliss, the Great Vajra?
How was what is called a maṇḍala taught,
And what is its ripening like?
Through laying out the lines and through examination,
How was accomplishment taught?
The bliss of the five secrets,
How did it come about, and what is it like?
How are the lords of the maṇḍala
And the manifestation of the families?
The reality of all the empowerments,
The reality of the assembly of hand emblems,
The great offering of summoning and drawing in all,
The great practice of self-blessing and the rest,
That reality and that secret,
And the unfailing, the supreme itself:
In order to accomplish the unchanging excellent,
Please teach all of us."

Thus was spoken the supreme sūtra of the Blessed One, of all the tathāgatas.

Then the Teacher, the Vajra Holder,
The lord of all the rites,
Having heard the host of doubts,
Spoke this answer.

Here, if one asks what the tantra of the meaning of "rite" is:

Among phenomena free of conceptual thought,
Because the aim of beings is fully examined,
It is therefore called a "rite",
Because conception is completely purified.

Then, if one asks what the later tantra is:

Tantra is explained as "continuum".
The later arose truly together with the earlier.
That reality, the secret of the secret,
Together with the later, is held to be tantra.

Here, if one asks what the tantra called "the first state" is:

Abiding fully in all of space,
The supreme nature of the vajra, the All-Supreme,
In that city of Great Bliss and great joy,
Is the unchanging supreme.

Thus the Blessed One Vajrasattva spoke this, which is called the reality of the rites of all rites.

Here, if one asks what Vajrasattva without beginning or end is:

Because its arising bears the mark of space,
It is supreme, without beginning or end.
The nature of the great being and the vajra
Is spoken of as Vajrasattva.

Then, if one asks who the Blessed One is:

Of the great accomplishment of the All-Supreme,
Maheśvara, the god above the gods,
The king of all the Vajra Holders,
Supreme delight, unchanging, supreme.

Here, if one asks how the Blessed One is Great Joy itself:

Faultless and utterly permanent,
Passionately attached to all desire,
That very one, through this, is this secret:
Great desire and great joy.

Here, if one asks how Samantabhadra is:

Utterly pure, supreme of all,
Liberated from the very beginning, the Tathāgata,
Samantabhadra, the self of all,
The supreme ritual of the bodhisattvas.

Here, if one asks how the Blessed One, the lord of Vajra Pride, is:

Of the great accomplishment of the All-Supreme,
The supreme mudrā of Maheśvara,
Because he is greatly praised as Vajra Īśvara,
He is the lord of the lords of Vajra Pride.

This is the Blessed One, the Person of Paramādya.

This reality of Paramādya:
Whatever Vajrasattva proclaims,
The riches of all the accomplishments,
All of them will be accomplished in it.

Thus the Blessed One Vajrasattva spoke the supreme reality.

Then, after that, the Blessed One Vajrasattva said: "I shall explain, through the performance of action, how the Blessed One is the supreme of all."

Here, if one asks how the Blessed One is the being of all:

Inconceivable and vast,
The profound reality, all-blissful, unchanging,
Pervading all and stainless,
The mind of awakening is the steadfast self.

Then the special action of this:

By contemplating the very steadfastness of mind itself,
One rests evenly in that samādhi.
From consciousness, all is vajra;
From the vajra, it becomes the holder.

If one asks how the Blessed One is the Vajra Holder:

The application of the being's blessing
Is to accomplish the supreme blessing of oneself.
If one practices it every day, by that
One will attain the very state of Vajrasattva.

Here, if one asks what the conduct of the Blessed One Samantabhadra is:

Because even through sattva, rajas and tamas
The aim of beings comes about,
Therefore I am Samantabhadra,
Following the conduct of awakening.

Here, if one asks how the Blessed One is Māra himself, and how, through taming, he is the victor:

Mind itself alone is Māra;
Mind itself is the Tathāgata.
In order to tame my own mind,
I have become Māra himself.

Here, if one asks how the Blessed One is perfect and complete awakening, and the Buddha:

Because one discerns one's own mind,
The supreme mind of awakening is the vajra itself.
Therefore that very one is the Buddha himself,
Because the supreme blessing of oneself is accomplished.

Here, if one asks how the Blessed One is Vajrapāṇi:

Indivisible and discerning,
He utterly subdues all the vicious.
The reality of all the buddhas is the vajra;
The mind of awakening is the steadfast self.
Into the hand of Samantabhadra
That weapon was given by the Victors,
In order to accomplish all that is steadfast.
Therefore he is Vajrapāṇi.

Here, if one asks what the tantra of knowing what arose from the first family is:

The perfection of wisdom is the mother;
The father is skill in means.
For the sake of those whom Vajrasattva tames,
The families came forth.

Here, if one asks how the Blessed One is desire and liberation:

The Blessed One is great attachment,
Because he is passionately attached to all accomplishments.
All accomplishments, the unchanging bliss,
Are proclaimed as Great Bliss.

Here, if one asks how the Blessed One is threefold:

Because I am endless in the three times,
Because I tame the three existences,
The supreme of three, for all the three worlds,
I am the three worlds, I am the three realms.

Here, if one asks how the Blessed One is all things:

I myself am steadfast, the bliss of all,
Because I am great, supreme in all merit.
Because what moves is compounded by mind,
I am all illusions.

Thus Vajrasattva spoke the reality of the performance of his own action.

Here, if one asks how one knows the arising of the first song:

For the supreme accomplishment of Maheśvara,
Who has fully accomplished existence,
And so that the Radiant One may act for the benefit of beings,
The Blessed One spoke it at the beginning of the eon.

Then, if one asks what it is:

Great desire, the Blessed One,
Supreme delight, unchanging, supreme:
For the sake of secrecy it is proclaimed as the heart.
The syllable hūṃ is the unchanging supreme.

Then, as for knowing
The reality of the near heart:
That reality, secret and secret,
The heart should be fully proclaimed.
What is accomplished in following the heart
Is called the near heart itself.

Here, if one asks what the explanatory tantra of the samaya of Vajra Amogha of Great Bliss is:

The unchanging bliss of all accomplishments
Is proclaimed as Great Bliss.
The vajra is that reality, accomplished unfailingly;
The samaya is hard to transgress.

Thus the Blessed One Vajrasattva spoke the song of the supreme and excellent.

Here, if one asks how one knows the meaning of the maṇḍala:

How the abodes and the wheels are,
How the beings are to be set in place,
And whatever placement there is together with the retinue:
Such a wheel is emanated by the Supreme.
Then, as for consciousness:
By directly seeing the wheel
Of the deities, Vajrasattva and the rest,
Whatever permanent merit there is,
So it is too if one draws it outwardly.

Then the rite of laying the lines:

The wise one draws the maṇḍala.
Focusing on the equality of all directions,
By laying four lines, to the east and west, the north and south,
There are eight maṇḍalas.
Encircled all around by the vajra line,
Beautified by eight pillars,
It is adorned with four gates, portals and arches,
With nets, half-nets and the rest.

There, as for knowing the meaning of the lord, if one asks what Vajrasattva is like:

Because its arising bears the mark of space,
It is supreme, without beginning or end.
The nature of the great Vajrasattva
Is spoken of as Śrī Paramādya.

Then, if one asks who that Blessed One is:

Of the great accomplishment of the All-Supreme,
Maheśvara, the god above the gods,
The king of all the Vajra Holders,
Supreme delight, unchanging, supreme.

Thus spoke the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

Then, through Avalokiteśvara:
Like the padmarāga ruby,
All-pure and unsurpassed,
Vajrasattva, one's own very self,
Is Avalokiteśvara himself.

Then the reality of the lotus family:

In order fully to accomplish the vajra Dharma,
In order to purify all beings,
The samādhi and wisdom
Of the pure family of the great lotus are gathered in full.

So it is said.

Here, if one asks how Ākāśagarbha is:

All is space, the Blessed One;
Seeing the merit of beings, and great,
Is Ākāśagarbha, the being.
Then, of the jewel family:
Empowerment and treasure,
Vajraketu and the great offering,
The accomplishment of the family of the great jewel,
Is the complete fulfillment of all wishes.

Thus the Blessed One Vajrasattva spoke the great reality of all the maṇḍalas.

Knowing the empowerment of all the vajra families:

For the Buddha, the jewel and the half-vajra;
For Vajraratna, the vajra;
For Avalokiteśvara, the vajra body;
For the jewel, the one who holds the jewel.

Then the reality of the hand emblem of Paramādya:

The great weapon of Maheśvara,
Of the great accomplishment of the All-Supreme,
The great reality of the five secrets,
Is called the five-pronged vajra.

Then, knowing the reality of the hand emblems of all the families:

For all the buddhas, the buddha itself;
For the various deeds, the Vajra Bearer;
For Lokeśvara, the vajra Dharma;
For the jewel, the heaped jewels.

Thus the Blessed One Vajrasattva spoke the reality of the hand emblems of all the empowerments.

Here, if one asks what the tantra of summoning all is:

By the great secret of all the rites,
With the vajra hook together with the bell,
One summons, and then the subsequent practice
Is done by the bell itself.

If one asks what the ritual of all the offerings is:

By the great secret of all the rites,
Having made offering with supreme delight,
With the magical displays of song and music
One makes offering, and again makes offering in yoga.

Here, if one asks what the reality of the great samaya of blessing of all accomplishments is:

By the great secret of all the rites,
Well blessed by all the mudrās,
Through this very great samaya,
Having accomplished oneself, one recites the heart mantra.
The being without beginning or end
Is Vajrasattva, great joy,
Samantabhadra, the self of all,
The lord of the lords of Vajra Pride.

This is the Person of Paramādya.

Here, if one asks what the tantra of the great practice of all one's own supreme deities is:

The stable and the moving,
Whatever is to be accomplished through all the rites,
Through the yoga of one's own supreme deity,
All of these should be accomplished.

Then the tantra of accomplishing everything subsequently:

Having taken up the vajra and bell,
Ringing the vajra bell well,
By subsequently accomplishing all the rites,
The supreme and the rites of action and the rest will be accomplished.

Here, if one asks what the tantra of accomplishing all without effort is:

Having accomplished whatever is to be accomplished, as one wishes,
Afterward, as one wishes,
Engaging in whatever conduct one wishes,
Doing everything and eating everything,
Whether standing or sitting,
Or walking about wherever one may be,
Whether laughing or speaking,
Whatever there is, it is just so.
Whatever comes into the range of whatever sense,
Make each into the nature of that.
Having rested evenly, the yogin
Should apply the yoga of his own supreme deity.
Through this very yoga
All things should be accomplished.
The reality of all the buddhas, all of it,
Will always be seen and accomplished.
That very great samaya:
Whatever wise one reads it with faith,
Being free of all wrongdoing,
Will become the lord of all accomplishments.

This is the reality of the great samaya.

Thus the ritual of Śrī Paramādya, supreme in equality, the guru, has been made clear.
The ritual for accomplishing the Supremely Secret was requested, for the swift accomplishment of supreme bliss.
Blessed by the sugatas and their heirs, together with the supreme vajra holders,
It was spoken by the excellent Great Bliss, the Vajra Holder, the supreme Great Joy.

The ritual of the Supremely Secret, from Śrī Paramādya.

Then the Teacher, the Vajra Holder,
Endless like space, going everywhere,
Having been praised by all the buddhas,
Spoke the meaning of the culmination of the rites.

This, together with my heart, is the great supreme.
Supreme of the supreme Great Bliss, and great attachment,
The constant source of the all-supreme beings,
This lord of all-supreme riches is what nourishes.
That which is endless, without beginning and without end,
The great way of all the great objects of offering,
Equal to space, I ask for the increase of its supreme reality.
That cannot be brought to an end by anyone.
Beings cling to what is lesser and delight in little,
Lacking faith, deceitful, and untrained,
Ever attached, and attached even to freedom from attachment:
In order to benefit them, this is the culmination.

In whose house this abides, or whoever fully practices it,
Or whoever always practices it here just as it was taught,
Through the power of his own direct experience, in every way,
The hosts of Māra's deeds cannot harm him.
Whoever accomplishes the bodies of Vajradhara and the rest,
For images and all the rest were taught here just as they are,
He is endowed with the ability to experience all of them directly;
The hosts of Māra's deeds cannot harm him.
Whoever, as he wishes, accomplishes all of these,
The vajra and the rest, the images and the rest,
Having drawn them, with intense desire, from the syllable hūṃ,
By that the accomplishment he desires is granted and attained.
As for the purification brought about by the kings,
For one in whom every aim is pure, there is nothing it will not bring about.
In this there are not even any obstructing māras.
Through realization, accomplishment increases and is blessed.
I do not become Māra himself; I do not become unsteady.
Because of steadfastness itself, I am the one who makes steadfast.
Whoever accomplishes this permanent great supreme,
For him I swiftly bring about accomplishment.

Toward the great accomplishment all become passionately attached, and it becomes utterly pleasing, utterly steadfast, utterly abundant, and ever vast.

Whoever practices the heart of the supreme king,
All the Victors rejoice, and he delights the Victors.
They attain great renown, and they attain bliss as well.
Therefore one should have faith in this and hold it in reverence.

Then the Victors said "Excellent!" to the Blessed One:

"Supreme father, heir of the buddhas, excellent, excellent!
Supreme heir, heir of the buddhas, excellent, excellent!
Supreme vajra, source of vajras, excellent!
Very self of the sacred Dharma, excellent, excellent!
Truly blessed by the buddhas,
Ever unchanging, the great unfailing accomplishment:
Just as was the first accomplishment,
So later too it will be accomplished in the same way."

Having thus spoken these great words,
The buddhas together with their heirs
Rejoiced in the speech
Of the great Vajrasattva.

The Supreme of All Kings of Rites, from Śrī Paramādya, is complete.

At Tholing, the Heart of the Ground of Sacred Commitment, it was translated, edited and finalized by the Indian preceptor, the paṇḍita and great scholar Mantrakalaśa, and the translator, the divine sovereign, the monk and lama Zhiwa Ö.

When the great translator Rinchen Zangpo
Translated this tantra of Śrī Paramādya,
Since in places no manuscript could be found, those parts were left untranslated.
I sought the manuscripts with effort, found them, and translated them.

This concludes the translation. The following are translator's notes.

The construction of the long closing sentences is loose in the Tibetan; the one consecrated with the diadem is taken to be the Blessed One himself, described by the epithets that precede. ཁམས་གསུམ་ཐམས་ཅད་ཀྱི་དབང་ཕྱུག་ཆེན་པོ is rendered as "great sovereignty over all the three realms," not as the god Maheśvara.  The deity in front, རྡོ་རྗེ་གསང་བ་ཡིད་ལས་བྱུང་བ, is rendered literally as "the Vajra Secret Born from Mind," since its Sanskrit form cannot be fixed with confidence. The closing words རྡོ་རྗེ་བྲན་ནོ ("vajra servant(s)") are terse; they may label the four heart mantras or only the last of them. The names of the four offering goddesses are given in English.  The glossary's ritual sense "urine" is followed for དྲི་ཆུ in the vase set before Vajrapāṇi; the word could also be read as scented water. ཆགས་བྲལ in the whispered verses is read as "dispassion," the state the disciple is urged not to return to. The meaning of ཕྲ་མོ་དབབ་པ ("the subtle will also descend") is unclear. དྲུག་ལྡན is taken as the musical mode ṣaḍja.  The opening four lines are the verse of dismissal addressed to the deities at the close of the rite. "Having exercised his overpowering" renders རྣམ་པར་གནོན་པར་མཛད་ནས literally; its object is unstated. The sentence beginning "The king of the accomplishment of the Great Bliss Vajra" appears to be a section label and has been kept as a standalone line. The phrase "a bodhisattva who is a great Vajradhara" takes རྡོ་རྗེ་འཆང་ཆེན་པོ་བྱང་ཆུབ་སེམས་དཔའ as describing the practitioner; it could also be read as an address to the great Vajradhara. The heart-mantra names in the final paragraph (Ūrṇā, Unshakable One, Embodied One, Elixir, and so on) are rendered from the Tibetan; "Unshakable One" may stand for Akṣobhya or Acala. དྲུག་ལྡན is taken as ṣaḍja, the first note of the Indian musical scale.  ཕར་མེན་མ is read as ཕྲ་མེན་མ ("sorceresses"), and རབ་ཏུ་ཞིབ ("fully pacifying" fevers) as རབ་ཏུ་ཞི་བ; both are evident scribal slips. The names in this list are the names of heart mantras and are rendered from the Tibetan; for many of them the Sanskrit original is not certain. "Harmful spirits of misfortune" renders བྱུར. "In the sky" in the item on trees follows the Tibetan ནམ་མཁར as written. Following the house glossary, བདེ་མཆོག is given as Saṃvara; in this list it could also simply mean "supreme bliss."  The rite names in the opening list are names of heart mantras, rendered from the Tibetan. "In the pose of pride" renders བསྙེམས་འཛིན་པ; "palms joined at the crown" takes སྤྱིར as "at the crown of the head," though it could mean "generally." The "Tathāgata whose command is meaningful and unobstructed" at the end probably refers to Amoghasiddhi, but the Tibetan gives a descriptive epithet rather than the standard name, so it is kept as such.  "Vajra fangs bared over the lower lip" interprets the difficult phrase རྡོ་རྗེ་མཆེ་བ་གཙིགས་པས་མ་མཆུའི་སྒྲོས་རྣམ་པར་གནོན་པའི་ཞལ. The names of the rites in the lists are heart-mantra names, rendered from the Tibetan. The "Tathāgata Īśvara" (དབང་ཕྱུག་དེ་བཞིན་གཤེགས་པ) at the end, master of the three realms, is introduced as the lord of the jewel family.  མགྲིན་འབྱོར་ཕྱིར in the opening verse is obscure (literally "for the sake of throat-wealth"); it is rendered "for the sake of prosperity" as the most plausible sense. "What Lies Beneath" renders རྡོ་རྗེ་རིན་ཆེན་གྱིས་འོག, which seems to be a slip for ས་འོག ("underground"), as in the next item. The weapon སེ་རལ་ཁ is taken as the khaṭvāṅga staff, which is uncertain. The lines on contempt are translated as the Tibetan stands ("whoever has held them in contempt should enter again and again"), read as a remedy for that fault. "Sattva, rajas, and tamas" are the three guṇas, here paired with the three gods.  "Joyful" renders དགའ་བོ, which could also be read as the name Nanda; in this description of the four gate figures it appears to qualify them rather than name a single being. The clause "and they are won" (འཐོབ་བོ, literally "obtain") is terse; it may mean that the practitioner gains mastery over the possessing nāgas. The verse line "If one has passed beyond the truth, this world of the living endures forever" is rendered literally; its sense is obscure.  "Supreme Bliss" renders བདེ་རབ, the name of a bodhisattva I cannot identify with confidence, so it is translated. In the vajra song, "you have come forth" renders རབ་ཏུ་བྱུང་བ, which here seems to mean "arisen" rather than monastic renunciation. The couplet "The Vajra Holder, maker of all, looked upon those who die through their karma" is terse, and it is unclear whether it is the content of his reply or narration.  "The Unfailing and Unimpeded" renders དོན་ཡོད་ཅིང་ཐོགས་པ་མེད་པ, which may be an epithet of the deity of this maṇḍala (cf. Amoghasiddhi) rather than a plain description; I have translated it. "It is not a matter of purification and of reliance" is a literal rendering of a terse sentence whose sense seems to be that no preliminary purification or support is needed. The description of the hand gestures is very compressed, and some details of finger placement are uncertain.  "Me" in the first paragraph renders བདག, taken as the speaking deity; it could also be read as "the Lord". The descriptions of the hand gestures are highly compressed, and the exact finger positions are often uncertain. "The tip of the sign" and "the womb" render རྟགས and སྐྱེ་གནས, the male and female organs in this yogic context. "In a raised manner" renders སྟོད་པ, which might instead be "praising" (bstod pa).  This passage is a terse manual of hand gestures, and many finger positions and their sequence are uncertain. "The stirring of joy" reads དཀའ་བ ("difficulty") as a slip for དགའ་བ ("joy"). "Vajra Born from Mind" renders རྡོ་རྗེ་ཡིད་ལས་བྱུང་བ, a goddess name I cannot identify with confidence.  "The five circles" (dkyil 'khor lnga) refers to the five points of the body that touch the ground in a full prostration. The extremely terse sentences in the karma mudrās of the Lotus and Jewel families are given as they stand, each naming a hand position or its placement.  "Rite" renders rtog pa (Skt. kalpa), which also means "conceptual thought"; the verses play on both senses, saying the rite arises from nonconceptual absorption and purifies conceptual thought. The last verse of the piece is obscure in the Tibetan and is translated as it stands.  "Vajra Opener" renders rdo rje 'byed ma, apparently the name of an offering goddess; the Sanskrit form could not be identified with confidence. The long prose sentence introducing the tantra of the first heart mantra is a dense chain of epithets in the Tibetan, and its construal here is approximate.  In "the action of the vajra mudrā" in the sentence on mātṛkās and ḍākinīs, the Tibetan reads lan ("answer"), evidently a slip for las ("action"), as in the parallel sentence just before it; the evident reading is translated. "Prasena divination" renders pra dbab, the rite of bringing a divinatory vision down into a mirror or other support.  The passage is a terse ritual description of hand gestures performed with the middle vajra, that is, the middle fingers; many of its operations are elliptical, and they have been rendered as literally as English allows. "The body of the middle vajra" follows the Tibetan as written, though it may be a slip for "the actions of the middle vajra" (lus for las), as in the parallel sentences. "The wheel" set at the heart is a hand gesture here, not a drawn diagram. In the chapter colophon, brgyud is read as rgyud, "tantra."  Here rdo rje bsnyems pa is read as "vajra pride," the posture in which the vajra is brandished, as the later verse "holding the posture of vajra pride" shows. It is not taken as the goddess Vajragarvā. The lines "it does not itself impose discipline" (literally "does not itself do taming or discipline") are obscure, and the rendering is tentative. Maheśvara in the tantra's title may be an epithet of the presiding deity rather than Śiva.  The names of the four inner goddesses are given as the Sanskrit forms their Tibetan names usually render: Vajrarāgā (rdo rje chags ma), Vajravilāsinī (rdo rje rol pa), Vajrasmṛti (rdo rje dran ma) and Vajrakāmeśvarī. The setting-in-place mantras of the first two do not match these names exactly, so the first two identifications are tentative. In "the crests are flowers," the written rtag ("permanent") is read as tog ("crest"), as in the parallel list in the previous piece. The four season goddesses are spring (honey, madhu), the rains (clouds), autumn and winter, matching their mantras.  "The praise beginning with śara" renders dang po sha ra la sogs pa literally. Which praise this means is unclear, since the hymn of one hundred and eight names that follows begins differently. Vajrasattva is invoked here as Śrī Paramādya, the deity who gives the text its title.  In "Some people who stay in gatherings," the Tibetan 'du gnang gnas pa is obscure, and the rendering is tentative. "Holding the eye of Vajrapāṇi" renders mig 'dzin pa literally. In the vajra-name rite, the vocative ཀྱེ ("O") corresponds to "he" in the Sanskrit formula.  The phrase rendered "saying stvaṃ with the mind" reads སེམས་སྟྭཾ; སེམས may be a Tibetan rendering of part of a mantra (as in the familiar pledge formula with sattva or samaya), so the sense is uncertain. Ridhita and gaṇḍe appear to be Sanskrit terms for musical or vocal ornamentation and kinds of song; they are left untranslated. "Endowed with the sense objects" renders ཡུལ་དང་ལྡན, which in this context may imply practice with the sense pleasures or with a consort.  "Secondary poison" renders ཉེ་བའི་དུག (Skt. upaviṣa), here the name of a ritual means or deity of the vajra dance; likewise the other phrases after "of the vajra dance" and "of the first vajra" appear to name particular mudrās or deity forms. Prasena is a form of divination in which a deity is made to descend into a mirror, a child, or the like and give answers.  "The great Maker of Men" renders སྐྱེས་བུ་བྱེད་པ་ཆེན་པོ (perhaps Skt. puruṣakāra), the name of a vajra-dance form; it could not be identified with confidence. The lines on Śakra having no wheel and becoming "the cause of my own son" are obscure in the Tibetan and are rendered literally. "The Lord of Desire" renders འདོད་པའི་དབང་ཕྱུག (Kāmeśvara).  "The Lord, the very substance of all" renders the somewhat obscure དེ་ཚེ་ཉིད་ཀྱི་དངོས་པོ་ཀུན་གཙོ་བོས; the subject of the supplication is not certain. "Lords of the hosts" renders ཚོགས་ཀྱི་དབང་ཕྱུག (perhaps Gaṇeśvara), which may be meant either as an epithet of Gaṇapati or as a separate group.  "The eleven were bound, surely conquered" renders the obscure ངེས་རྒྱལ་བཅུ་གཅིག; the eleven are probably the eleven Rudras, but the phrase is uncertain. In "intent on brandishing the vajra," གསོལ་བ is read as གསོར་བ ("brandish"), as the text writes it later. ཀླུ་མཁན་མ is read as གླུ་མཁན་མ, "female singers." ཧཱུྃ་མདད is read as ཧཱུྃ་མཛད. The line ཅི་བཞིན་སྐུ་ནི་དགེ་འདུན་དུ ("with the body as is fitting, amid the Saṅgha") is obscure and translated literally. "The three brothers" (མིང་པོ་གསུམ) is translated literally and not identified.  བདེ་སོགས་མ, the consort of Śakra seized in the rite, is read as བདེ་སྒྲོགས་མ, Śacī. ནོར་སྦྱིན ("Giver of Wealth") is taken as Dhanada, an epithet of Kubera, and སྐེམ་བྱེད as Skanda. ཁྲོ་བོ་ཆེན་པོ་སྣ་ཚོགས་གྲུབ་པ is rendered by its meaning, "the Great Wrathful One of Manifold Accomplishment"; it may name a mudrā or a form of the deity. The line ཐབས་མེད་པས་ནི་ཐབས་ཡིན་ནོ ("through no means at all, is itself the means") is cryptic and is translated literally.  སངས་རྒྱས་སྐྲ ("buddha hairs") is read as སངས་རྒྱས་སྐུ, "buddha forms." རྡོ་རྗེར་བ is read as རྡོ་རྗེ་ར་བ, "vajra fence." ཞི་བ in the list of family deities is taken as Śiva. Several medical terms (རྐང་ཤུ, ཆུའི་དམུ་རྫིང, མཚན་བར་རྡོལ་བ, ཟད་བྱེད) are given their usual senses ("sores of the feet," "dropsy," "fistula," "wasting disease"), and the exact conditions meant are not certain. ཕྲ་མེན is rendered "witches." The names of the four Great Lotus forms that perform the rites are translated by their meaning.  བཞུ་གསལ in "make a clear request" is read as ཞུ་གསལ. The names of the four beings around Ākāśagarbha (Empowerment, Treasure, the Great Unsurpassed Jewel, Perfect Glory of Prosperity) are translated by their meaning; their Sanskrit forms are not certain. "The Sky-Born" (མཁའ་སྐྱེས) is taken as an epithet of Ākāśagarbha.  གཡུང་དྲུང, which stands beside Brahmā and Rudra, is rendered "the Eternal One." It apparently stands for Viṣṇu, perhaps as Acyuta, and it matches the earlier mention of "the three brothers." The names of the four sisters (Subhagā, Śivā, Vārāhī, Siddhā) are reconstructed from the Tibetan and the mantra syllables. The epithets in the line "the Peaceful, the Terrifying, likewise Gold, the Accomplished and the Peaceful" are translated literally. The line བཞི་པོ་ངག་ནི་རིམ་པ་བཞིན ("the four in sequence, as stated") is obscure.  The goddess named "Sky Vajra" (Tibetan mkha' rdo rje) is rendered literally, as its Sanskrit form cannot be fixed with confidence; her mantra invokes the sky (gagana). "Kindle all poisons" renders sbar ba literally; the sense may be "activate" or "apply" poisons. "The one who holds the nāgas" (klu 'dzin pa) may refer to the practitioner as nāga-charmer.  In "the supremely secret persons" the Tibetan reads mi ("persons"); the following line calls these "secret names," so ming ("names") is read. The four consort names are given in reconstructed Sanskrit from their Tibetan forms (Kāmavajrī, Vijayavajrī, Vajrakāmeśvarī); "Vajra Pride" and "the Vajra Proud One" render rdo rje bsnyems pa and rdo rje bsnyems ma literally. The verse on the four goddesses and the description of the attendant figures are terse, and the attribution of some details to particular goddesses is interpretive.  The verse beginning "Having fully entered, with the left" is terse; "the left" likely refers to the left hand or the left side of the practitioner's posture. The four lines of the samaya song each open with one of the syllables jaḥ, hūṃ, vaṃ, hoḥ, matching the four actions of summoning, drawing in, binding and controlling.  "The great enclosure of lotuses" renders padma'i ra ba che; the second occurrence reads padma ra ba, which may instead be intended as "padmarāga" (ruby), giving "the great ruby."  "The group of those who are certain" renders ངེས་པའི་ཕུང་པོ (Skt. niyatarāśi), the class of beings destined for awakening; here ཕུང་པོ means "group", not "aggregate". The line rendered "Who makes no boast of his virtue" (དགེ་བ་རབ་ཏུ་མི་སྤྱོམས་པ) is somewhat uncertain in sense.  "The Lotus Essence" translates པདྨའི་སྙིང་པོ, which here names the lotus family's figure among the five syllables; I could not identify it with confidence as a fixed Sanskrit name (possibly Padmagarbha), so it is rendered literally. "Cymbals" for མཉམ is a probable sense in this list of instruments.  "The maṇḍala of all the wicked ones" renders སྡིག་པ་ཐམས་ཅད་ཀྱི་དཀྱིལ་འཁོར; the sense of སྡིག་པ here (wicked beings to be subdued) is not certain. The four-line verse "Just as, having dwelt in the first house ... Not everything is accomplished in that way" is obscure and is translated literally. In the lotus-family song, the words for "fierce," "infinite" and "peaceful" may also play on the names Rudra, Ananta and Śiva used elsewhere in this text.  The colors of the four ḍākinīs are obscure in the Tibetan, particularly "white and gazing" for the third, and are translated literally. The instruction "six times, with ... in between" renders the terse དྲུག་ལྡན་བར་མའི; its exact sense for the recitation is uncertain. "The one who leans to the left" translates གཡོན་བསྙེམས་མ, which I could not identify as a fixed name.  "The tip of the All-Supreme" in the mudrā description presumably names a finger, but which one is not stated. "Vajrāmṛta" renders རྡོ་རྗེ་བདུད་རྩི, here heading the list of māras; its identification is uncertain. The second goddess's name ends in དམ་ཚིག་ལན, rendered "Answer of the Pledge"; the sense is unclear. "Raiser of the Dead" renders འཆི་བ་སློང་བ, which might also mean "she who rouses death."  "Great blood" and "camphor" in the samaya of the supreme yoga are the customary tantric code terms for menstrual blood and semen. In the line "by seeing it one becomes invisible," the text reads གང་ཞིང, taken here as a slip for གང་ཞིག ("that which"). The line "Refraining, and discerning each thing" (མི་བྱེད་པ་དང་སོ་སོར་རྟོག) is terse, and its exact sense is uncertain. So is "the supreme ones that the Teacher reveals" (སྟོན་པའི་མཆོག་ཏུ་གྱུར་པ).  Raudrī is taken as the Sanskrit for དྲག་མོ, the fierce goddess named beside Bhṛkuṭī in Avalokiteśvara's retinue. The goddesses in Ākāśagarbha's wheel of light are given in English, since their Sanskrit forms cannot be identified with confidence: Empowerment, Treasure, Supreme Victor, Sovereign and Great Offering. In "What is not to be given should be given" (སྦྱིན་མིན་རྣམས་ནི་སྦྱིན་བྱ), the Tibetan is terse. It may instead mean that one should give even to those who are not proper recipients.  Here དབང་ཕྱུག་ཆེན་པོ ("great lord," the same words as the name Maheśvara) is an epithet of the accomplishment and of the practitioner, not the god Śiva. It is therefore rendered "great sovereign." ལྷག་པའི་ལྷ (Skt. adhidevatā, the presiding deity of one's practice) is rendered "chosen deity." In the heading of the extensive special rite, ཤན་ཏུ is read as ཤིན་ཏུ ("utterly"). The closing string of epithets runs on into the next piece.  Here དབང་ཕྱུག་ཆེན་པོ and དབང་ཕྱུག ("great lord," "lord") are epithets of the state that is attained, not the god Śiva or Īśvara. They are therefore rendered "great sovereign" and "sovereign." The triad ཐམས་ཅད་རྣལ་འབྱོར, རྣལ་འབྱོར་ཆེན་པོ and ཤིན་ཏུ་རྣལ་འབྱོར ("all-yoga, great yoga, and utmost yoga") seems to correspond to sarvayoga, mahāyoga and atiyoga.  In the description of the vajra bond, ཀུན་མཆོག is read as "all the tips" (Skt. sarvāgra, with mchog for agra), not as the epithet the All-Supreme. ལྕགས་ཀྱུ is likewise the hook formed with the fingers, not the goddess Aṅkuśī. Also, གདོན་མི་ཟ་བར is the idiom "without fail" and has nothing to do with grahas. The mudrā descriptions are terse, and their finer points of finger placement are uncertain. In the empowerment, རྡོ་རྗེ་བདུད་རྩི is taken as the vajra nectar of the consecrated water, though it could also be the deity Vajrāmṛta. སྒེག་མོ is rendered Lāsyā, the goddess of graceful charm.  The musical terms ribita, "six-note" (perhaps the hexatonic ṣāḍava mode or the note ṣaḍja), grāma and rāga are technical terms of Indian music; their exact sense here is uncertain. The verb stod pa / bstod pa in the mudrā descriptions is rendered "brandishing"; it may instead mean "raising" or "praising," and the terse mudrā instructions throughout are elliptical, so their precise hand positions are uncertain. "The sign" (rtags) as a place on the body probably means the genitals.  "The third-day, the fourth-day, the recurring, the constant" most likely refers to kinds of fever (tertian, quartan, intermittent and continuous), pacified by the gaze. The clause about the fence of blazing vajras says, as written, that the great wrathful one "too is able to enter" it; one might expect a negative ("not even I can enter"), but the text is translated as it stands. The verb rendered "brandishing" (stod pa / bstod pa) may instead mean "raising." The terse mudrā instructions are elliptical throughout, and "tripatāka" (three-banner hand) and kataka are names of hand gestures. "Vajra Mindfulness" (rdo rje dran ma) is a literal rendering of a feminine mudrā or deity name I cannot identify with confidence.  The verb rendered "raising" or "brandishing" (stod pa) is uncertain in these mudrā descriptions. Ratnavajrī and Sattvavajrī render rin chen ma and sems ma rdo rje ma / sems dpa'i rdo rje ma, the goddesses of the jewel and sattva families; "Exalting" is a literal rendering of gzengs bstod pa, the name of a gesture. The short items at the end of the passage are elliptical headings in a list of mudrā applications and are translated as they stand.  The opening items continue an elliptical list of qualities (expression, dhāraṇī, grounds, discriminating knowledges, unshared qualities and so on) and are translated as headings. "Conformity of one's whole being" renders bdag nyid thams cad kyi rjes, whose precise sense is unclear. The clause stating that "the Blessed One too brings them under control" follows the instrumental as written; it could also mean that even the Blessed One is brought under control.  "Coin" renders dong tse, whose sense as a ritual implement here is uncertain. "Clinging to the Mountain" is a literal rendering of ri chags pa, a mudrā name I cannot identify. The long series of short items in the first paragraph are elliptical instructions in a list of wrathful mudrā applications and are translated as they stand.  The Tibetan reads mi ba ṭvāṃ ga after "karma mudrā"; this is taken as a garbled transcription of khaṭvāṅga, though the reading is uncertain. In the section on the thousands of mudrās, stong nyid ("emptiness" or "the thousand itself") is read as stong gnyis, "the second thousand", since the passage counts a first, third, and fourth thousand. The repeated phrase "mudrā that accomplishes" in the first of these sentences appears to be a duplication and is rendered once.  In the phrase "the agent endowed with the wrathful vajra gaze," the Tibetan reads dang bas pa, which is taken as a slip for ldan pa, "endowed with." In the verse on the Vajra Holder, kun gsol ba is rendered "asked for every accomplishment"; gsol could also be read as the honorific "partake of," and the sense is uncertain.  "Divinatory descents" renders pra dbab pa, the bringing down of a deity into a mirror or medium for divination (Skt. prasena).  "The descent" renders ltung ba, here in its yogic sense of the descent or fall of the vital essence rather than the monastic "downfall"; the same applies to "the descent is drawn up." In the list of rites, 'bru ("grain") is rendered "causing pustules," reading it as related to 'brum; the sense is uncertain. Near the end of the secret mudrās, bru(ng) pa is taken as brud pa, "rubbing," as in the preceding sentence.  "One should join them with the senses" renders དབང་པོ་རྣམས་དང་སྦྱར; the plural could alternatively mean "with the Indras", but the sense faculties fit the context of action mudrās better. The phrase "another hundred" reads རྒྱ་གཞན in the Tibetan as brgya ("hundred"), which the parallel lines plainly require. The closing line literally says that "the Blessed One spoke the supreme sūtra of all the tathāgatas"; since the questions are addressed to Vajradhara and are answered in the next section, it is rendered as a statement that this supreme sūtra was spoken. The mukuṇḍa, guñja and timili are musical instruments that could not be identified with confidence and are left in their transliterated forms.  The answer on "rite" plays on the Tibetan rtog pa, which means both a ritual manual (kalpa) and conceptual thought; the explanation turns on this double sense. "The families came forth" reads རིག as rigs, "family", which the question about the first family requires. The answer introduced as being about the "first song" corresponds to the earlier question about the first heart mantra; the Tibetan here reads "song", and that is kept.  The verse on the empowerments of the families (Buddha, Vajraratna, Avalokiteśvara, the jewel) is terse, and the assignment of emblems to each is given as the wording most plainly allows; the exact correspondence is uncertain. In "for the swift accomplishment of supreme bliss" the Tibetan bde mchog is taken as a common noun, not as the deity Cakrasaṃvara, which the context does not support. In the effortless-practice verses dbang po is taken as "sense faculty", not as Indra.  In the Victors' praise, don yod grub pa is taken as "the unfailing accomplishment", in keeping with the following lines on accomplishment, rather than as the name of the buddha Amoghasiddhi; both readings are possible. "The Heart of the Ground of Sacred Commitment" renders thugs dam sa'i snying po, evidently the name of the temple at Tholing where the translation was made. "The divine sovereign" renders lha btsan po, the royal title of Zhiwa Ö, a prince of the Gugé royal house who became a monk. The final verse is by an unnamed later reviser.

The following is a note about this translation.

This is an AI-generated translation from the AI Stupa project. It was produced by a careful, doctrine-aware process aiming at a high degree of accuracy, but it has not been reviewed by a human translator and may contain errors. It is offered as a provisional translation for those texts of the Tibetan Buddhist canon that have not yet received an authoritative human translation. Where a published translation exists — above all from 84000: Translating the Words of the Buddha (84000.co) — that translation is definitive and should be preferred; please consult it instead. These translations are released into the public domain (CC0) in the spirit of spreading the Dharma to all corners of the earth.

Just as the Bodhi tree now standing at Bodh Gayā neither is nor is not the same tree beneath which the Buddha attained awakening, so too this stupa is intended as the next generation of stupa — dependently arising according to the conditions of its environment, and ceaselessly proclaiming the Dharma for the benefit of all sentient beings.

May whoever encounters these words derive great benefit.
